<p id="mwIw"><b id="mwJA">Parasnath Hill</b> (also <b id="mwJQ">Sammet Shikhar</b>, <b id="mwJg">Marang Buru</b>) is a mountain peak in the Parasnath hill range.<sup about="#mwt28" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-SorenSingh2024_4-1" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"SorenSingh2024"}}'><a href="#cite_note-SorenSingh2024-4" id="mwJw"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwKA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwKQ">[</span>4<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwKg">]</span></span></a></sup> It is located towards the eastern end of the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chota_Nagpur_Plateau" title="Chota Nagpur Plateau" id="mwKw">Chota Nagpur Plateau</a> in the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giridih_district" title="Giridih district" id="mwLA">Giridih district</a> (<a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hazaribagh_district" title="Hazaribagh district" id="mwLQ">Hazaribagh district</a> in British India) of the Indian state of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jharkhand" title="Jharkhand" id="mwLg">Jharkhand</a>.<sup about="#mwt31" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Giridih_official_6-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Giridih_official"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-Giridih_official-6"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Giridih_official-6" id="mwLw"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwMA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwMQ">[</span>6<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwMg">]</span></span></a></sup> The hill is named after <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parshvanatha" title="Parshvanatha" class="mw-redirect" id="mwMw">Parshvanatha</a>, the 23rd <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jainism" title="Jainism" id="mwNA">Jain</a> <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tirthankara" title="Tirthankara" id="mwNQ">Tirthankara</a>, who attained liberation here in 8th century BCE.<sup about="#mwt34" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-giridih_3-1" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"giridih"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-giridih-3"}}'><a href="#cite_note-giridih-3" id="mwNg"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwNw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwOA">[</span>3<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwOQ">]</span></span></a></sup> In this connection, there is the holiest <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jainism" title="Jainism" id="mwOg">Jain</a> pilgrimage, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shikharji" title="Shikharji" id="mwOw">Shikharji</a> on the top of hill. The hill is also known as Sammet Shikhar (<abbr style="font-size:85%" title="literal translation" about="#mwt26" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:ExpandedAttrs" id="mwPQ" data-mw='{"attribs":[[{"txt":"title"},{"html":"&lt;span typeof=\"mw:Nowiki\" id=\"mwPA\">literal translation&lt;/span>"}]],"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"Literally","href":"./Template:Literally"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"great mountain"}},"i":0}}]}'>lit.</abbr><span style="white-space: nowrap;" about="#mwt26"><span typeof="mw:Entity"> </span></span><span class="gloss-quot" about="#mwt26">'</span><span class="gloss-text" about="#mwt26">great mountain</span><span class="gloss-quot" Owing to space constraints, the locations on click through to the larger map may vary slightly.</div></div></div><span class="mw-empty-elt" about="#mwt44"><link rel="mw:PageProp/Category" href="./Category:Articles_containing_OSM_location_maps"/></span>
<p id="mwVg">At <span about="#mwt47" typeof="mw:Transclusion" id="mwVw" data-mw='{"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"cvt","href":"./Template:Cvt"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"1365"},"2":{"wt":"m"}},"i":0}}]}'>1,365</span><span typeof="mw:Entity" about="#mwt47"> </span><span about="#mwt47">m (4,478</span><span typeof="mw:Entity" about="#mwt47"> </span><span about="#mwt47">ft)</span> Parasnath is the highest mountain peak in the state of Jharkhand, and is <i id="mwWA">theoretically</i> inter-visible (by direct line of sight on a perfectly clear day) with Mount Everest over <span about="#mwt48" typeof="mw:Transclusion" id="mwWQ" data-mw='{"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"cvt","href":"./Template:Cvt"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"450"},"2":{"wt":"km"}},"i":0}}]}'>450</span><span typeof="mw:Entity" about="#mwt48"> </span><span about="#mwt48">km (280</span><span typeof="mw:Entity" about="#mwt48"> </span><span about="#mwt48">mi)</span> away.<sup about="#mwt51" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-9" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-9"}}'><a href="#cite_note-9" id="mwWg"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwWw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwXA">[</span>9<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwXQ">]</span></span></a></sup> The foothills of the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Himalayas" title="Himalayas" id="mwXg">Himalayas</a> are about 180 miles away to the north over the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ganges" title="Ganges" id="mwXw">Ganges</a>.<sup about="#mwt54" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Annandale1912_10-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Annandale1912"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-Annandale1912-10"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Annandale1912-10" id="mwYA"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwYQ"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwYg">[</span>10<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwYw">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

<p id="mwZA">There is a Jain temple on the mountain peak known as "<b id="mwZQ">Svarna Bhadra koot "</b> ("cottage of golden grace").<sup about="#mwt57" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-11" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-11"}}'><a href="#cite_note-11" id="mwZg"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwZw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwaA">[</span>11<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwaQ">]</span></span></a></sup> The temple is made of marble. There is another marble Jain temple on hill, known as Jal Mandir.</p>

<p id="mwag">It is easily accessible from <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parasnath_railway_station" title="Parasnath railway station" id="mwaw">Parasnath railway station</a>.</p>

</section><section data-mw-section-id="2" id="mwbA" aria-labelledby="Geology"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Geology">Geology</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Parasnath&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section's source code: Geology"><span>edit source</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p id="mwbQ">The underlying rock is <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metamorphosed" title="Metamorphosed" class="mw-redirect" id="mwbg">metamorphosed</a> fine-grained <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sedimentary" title="Sedimentary" class="mw-redirect" id="mwbw">sedimentary</a> rock with <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Granodiorite" title="Granodiorite" id="mwcA">granodiorite</a> <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igneous_intrusion" title="Igneous intrusion" id="mwcQ">igneous intrusions</a> through these older formations.<sup about="#mwt60" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-12" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-12"}}'><a href="#cite_note-12" id="mwcg"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwcw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwdA">[</span>12<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwdQ">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

</section><section data-mw-section-id="3" id="mwdg" aria-labelledby="Fauna"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Fauna">Fauna</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Parasnath&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section's source code: Fauna"><span>edit source</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p id="mwdw">As originally described several landsnails were only found in the Parasnath Hills area but the fauna is otherwise typical for Bengal.<sup about="#mwt61" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Annandale1912_10-1" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Annandale1912"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Annandale1912-10" id="mweA"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mweQ"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mweg">[</span>10<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwew">]</span></span></a></sup></p> 

</section><section data-mw-section-id="4" id="mwfA" aria-labelledby="History"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="History">History</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Parasnath&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section's source code: History"><span>edit source</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<span class="mw-empty-elt" about="#mwt62" typeof="mw:Transclusion" id="mwfQ" data-mw='{"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"Jainism","href":"./Template:Jainism"},"params":{},"i":0}}]}'><style <p id="mwfw">Historically, Parasnath or Sammet Shikharji has been an ancient Jain pilgrimage site. It is a place where the 23rd tirthankara Parshvanath (also known as Parasnath) attained liberation here in 772 BCE. It is noteworthy that the name of the Parasnath hill derived from the Jain Tirthankar Parshvanath who is also known as Parasnath or Parsva who attained liberation here during the 8th century BCE. After preaching for 70 years, Parshvanath attained <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moksha_(Jainism)" title="Moksha (Jainism)" id="mwgA">moksha</a> at <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shikharji" title="Shikharji" id="mwgQ">Shikharji</a> on Parasnath hill. Some Jain texts name the place as Mount Sammeta.<sup about="#mwt67" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJacobi1964275_13-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTEJacobi1964275"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEJacobi1964275-13"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Jacobi"},"2":{"wt":"1964"},"p":{"wt":"275"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJacobi1964275-13" id="mwgg"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwgw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwhA">[</span>13<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwhQ">]</span></span></a></sup> It is the most revered in Jainism because 20 of its 24 <i id="mwhg">tirthankars</i> are believed to have attained moksha there.<sup about="#mwt70" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECort2010130–133_14-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTECort2010130–133"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTECort2010130–133-14"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"Sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Cort"},"2":{"wt":"2010"},"pp":{"wt":"130–133"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECort2010130–133-14" id="mwhw"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwiA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwiQ">[</span>14<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwig">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt73" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWiley2009148_15-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTEWiley2009148"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEWiley2009148-15"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Wiley"},"2":{"wt":"2009"},"p":{"wt":"148"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWiley2009148-15" id="mwiw"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwjA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwjQ">[</span>15<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwjg">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt76" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDundas2002221_16-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTEDundas2002221"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEDundas2002221-16"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Dundas"},"2":{"wt":"2002"},"p":{"wt":"221"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDundas2002221-16" id="mwjw"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwkA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwkQ">[</span>16<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwkg">]</span></span></a></sup> at the age of 100 on Shravana Shukla Saptami according to Lunar Calendar.<sup about="#mwt79" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDundas200230_17-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTEDundas200230"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEDundas200230-17"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Dundas"},"2":{"wt":"2002"},"p":{"wt":"30"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDundas200230-17" id="mwkw"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwlA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwlQ">[</span>17<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwlg">]</span></span></a></sup> His <i id="mwlw"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moksha_(Jainism)" title="Moksha (Jainism)" id="mwmA">moksha</a></i> (liberation from the cycle of birth and death) in Jain tradition<sup about="#mwt82" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESangave2001104_18-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTESangave2001104"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTESangave2001104-18"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Sangave"},"2":{"wt":"2001"},"p":{"wt":"104"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESangave2001104-18" id="mwmQ"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwmg"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwmw">[</span>18<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwnA">]</span></span></a></sup>  is celebrated as Moksha Saptami. This day is celebrated on large scale at Parasnath tonk of the mountain, in northern <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jharkhand" title="Jharkhand" id="mwnQ">Jharkhand</a>, part of the Parasnath Range<sup about="#mwt85" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKailash_Chand_Jain199113_19-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTEKailash Chand Jain199113"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEKailash_Chand_Jain199113-19"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Kailash Chand Jain"},"2":{"wt":"1991"},"p":{"wt":"13"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKailash_Chand_Jain199113-19" id="mwng"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwnw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwoA">[</span>19<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwoQ">]</span></span></a></sup> by offering Nirvana <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laddu" title="Laddu" id="mwog">Laddu</a> (Sugar balls) and reciting of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nirvana_Kanda" title="Nirvana Kanda" id="mwow">Nirvana Kanda</a>. Parshvanatha has been called <i id="mwpA"><span title="International Alphabet of Sanskrit transliteration" about="#mwt88" typeof="mw:Transclusion" id="mwpQ" data-mw='{"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"IAST","href":"./Template:IAST"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"purisādāṇīya"},"italic":{"wt":"yes"}},"i":0}}]}'><i lang="sa-Latn">purisādāṇīya</i></span></i> (beloved of the people) by Jains.<sup about="#mwt89" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJacobi1964271_with_footnote_1_20-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTEJacobi1964271 with footnote 1"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEJacobi1964271_with_footnote_1-20"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Jacobi"},"2":{"wt":"1964"},"p":{"wt":"271 with footnote 1"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJacobi1964271_with_footnote_1-20" id="mwpg"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwpw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwqA">[</span>20<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwqQ">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt92" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKailash_Chand_Jain199112–13_21-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTEKailash Chand Jain199112–13"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEKailash_Chand_Jain199112–13-21"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Kailash Chand Jain"},"2":{"wt":"1991"},"pp":{"wt":"12–13"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKailash_Chand_Jain199112–13-21" id="mwqg"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwqw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwrA">[</span>21<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwrQ">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt95" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESchubring1964220_22-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTESchubring1964220"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTESchubring1964220-22"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Schubring"},"2":{"wt":"1964"},"p":{"wt":"220"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchubring1964220-22" id="mwrg"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwrw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwsA">[</span>22<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwsQ">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

<p id="mwsg">The earliest literary reference to Shikharji (Parasnath) as a <i id="mwsw">tirth</i> (place of Jain pilgrimage) is found in the <i id="mwtA"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jnatrdharmakathah" title="Jnatrdharmakathah" class="mw-redirect" id="mwtQ">Jñātṛdhārmakātha</a></i>, one of the twelve core texts of Jainism compiled in 6th century BCE by chief disciple of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahavira" title="Mahavira" id="mwtg">Mahavira</a>. Shikharji is also mentioned in the <i id="mwtw">Pārśvanāthacarita</i>, a twelfth-century biography of Pārśva. A 13th century CE palm-leaf manuscript of <i id="mwuA"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalpa_Sūtra" title="Kalpa Sūtra" id="mwuQ">Kalpa Sūtra</a></i> and <i id="mwug">Kalakacaryakatha</i> has an image of a scene of Parshavanatha's <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nirvana" title="Nirvana" id="mwuw">nirvana</a> at Shikharji.<sup about="#mwt98" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEastman194395_23-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTEEastman194395"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEEastman194395-23"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Eastman"},"2":{"wt":"1943"},"p":{"wt":"95"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEastman194395-23" id="mwvA"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwvQ"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwvg">[</span>23<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwvw">]</span></span></a></sup>
This all indicates Parasnath was a Jain site since ancient times. Also many historians accept Parasnath as the place of Nirvan Kalyanak of the historical figure and the 23rd <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tirthankar" title="Tirthankar" class="mw-redirect" id="mwwA">Tirthankar</a>, Parshvanath.<sup about="#mwt101" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBalfour1885141_24-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTEBalfour1885141"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEBalfour1885141-24"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"Sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Balfour"},"2":{"wt":"1885"},"p":{"wt":"141"}},"i":0}}]}'><a id="mwxA">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt104" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDundas200230_17-1" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDundas200230-17" id="mwxQ"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwxg"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwxw">[</span>17<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwyA">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt107" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESangave2001103_25-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTESangave2001103"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTESangave2001103-25"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Sangave"},"2":{"wt":"2001"},"p":{"wt":"103"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESangave2001103-25" id="mwyQ"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwyg"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwyw">[</span>25<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwzA">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt110" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUniversity_of_Calcutta1845256_26-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTEUniversity of Calcutta1845256"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEUniversity_of_Calcutta1845256-26"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"University of Calcutta"},"2":{"wt":"1845"},"p":{"wt":"256"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUniversity_of_Calcutta1845256-26" id="mwzQ"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwzg"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwzw">[</span>26<span class="cite-bracket" id="mw0A">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

</section><section data-mw-section-id="5" id="mw0Q" aria-labelledby="Jain_heritage"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Jain_heritage">Jain heritage</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Parasnath&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section's source code: Jain heritage"><span>edit source</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<span class="mw-empty-elt" about="#mwt113" typeof="mw:Transclusion" id="mw0g" data-mw='{"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"Main","href":"./Template:Main"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Shikharji"}},"i":0}}]}'><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1368532237" about="#mwt114" typeof="mw:Extension/templatestyles" data-mw='{"name":"templatestyles","attrs":{"src":"Module:Hatnote/styles.css"},"body":{"extsrc":""}}'/></span><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable" about="#mwt113" id="mw0w">Main article: <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shikharji" title="Shikharji">Shikharji</a></div>
<p id="mw1A">Parasnath Hill, located in the state of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jharkhand" title="Jharkhand" id="mw1Q">Jharkhand</a>, India, is the highest mountain in the state and is considered a sacred site by followers of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jainism" title="Jainism" id="mw1g">Jainism</a>. The hill is named after <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parshvanatha" title="Parshvanatha" class="mw-redirect" id="mw1w">Parshvanatha</a>, the 23rd Tirthankara in Jain tradition. It is home to several Jain temples and attracts pilgrims from across the country. The recent legal developments concerning the preservation of its sanctity highlight the ongoing tensions between religious practices and environmental concerns. The <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jharkhand_High_Court" title="Jharkhand High Court" id="mw2A">Jharkhand High Court</a> has sought responses from both the central and state governments regarding a plea aimed at protecting the hill's sanctity, indicating the importance of this site in the context of religious and cultural heritage in India.<sup about="#mwt117" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-27" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-27"}}'><a href="#cite_note-27" id="mw2Q"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mw2g"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mw2w">[</span>27<span class="cite-bracket" id="mw3A">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

<p id="mw3Q">This is one of the most holy and revered sites for the Jain community. They call it Sammed Sikhar. It is a major pilgrimage site. Out of 24 <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tirthankaras" title="Tirthankaras" class="mw-redirect" id="mw3g">Tirthankaras</a> of Jains, twenty attained nirvana at Parshvnatha Hills.</p>

<p id="mw3w">On the mountain, there are the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shikharji" title="Shikharji" id="mw4A">Shikharji</a> <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jain_temple" title="Jain temple" id="mw4Q">Jain temples</a>, an important <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tirtha_(Jainism)" title="Tirtha (Jainism)" id="mw4g">tirthakshetra</a> or Jain pilgrimage site.<sup about="#mwt118" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-28" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-28"}}'><a href="#cite_note-28" id="mw4w"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mw5A"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mw5Q">[</span>28<span class="cite-bracket" id="mw5g">]</span></span></a></sup> For each Tirthankara there is a shrine (gumti or Tonk) on the hill.<sup about="#mwt121" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-29" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-29"}}'><a href="#cite_note-29" id="mw5w"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mw6A"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mw6Q">[</span>29<span class="cite-bracket" id="mw6g">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

<p id="mw6w">The Jain temple is believed to be constructed either by the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magadha_(Mahajanapada)" title="Magadha (Mahajanapada)" class="mw-redirect" id="mw7A">Magadha</a> King <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bimbisara" title="Bimbisara" id="mw7Q">Bimbisara</a> or by the Kalinga King Avakinnayo Karakandu.</p>

<p id="mw7g">An ancient idol of Parasnath is located in the valley at Palganj. The idol is believed to be 2500 years old.<sup about="#mwt124" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-30" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-30"}}'><a href="#cite_note-30" id="mw7w"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mw8A"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mw8Q">[</span>30<span class="cite-bracket" id="mw8g">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

<p id="mw8w">During 772 BCE at the age of 100 on Shravana Shukla Saptami according to Lunar Calendar.<sup about="#mwt125" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDundas200230_17-2" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTEDundas200230"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEDundas200230-17"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Dundas"},"2":{"wt":"2002"},"p":{"wt":"30"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDundas200230-17" id="mw9A"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mw9Q"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mw9g">[</span>17<span class="cite-bracket" id="mw9w">]</span></span></a></sup> Tirthankar Parswanath <i id="mw-A"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moksha_(Jainism)" title="Moksha (Jainism)" id="mw-Q">moksha</a></i> (liberation from the cycle of birth and death) in Jaina tradition<sup about="#mwt128" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESangave2001104_18-1" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTESangave2001104"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTESangave2001104-18"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Sangave"},"2":{"wt":"2001"},"p":{"wt":"104"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESangave2001104-18" id="mw-g"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mw-w"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mw_A">[</span>18<span class="cite-bracket" id="mw_Q">]</span></span></a></sup>  is celebrated as Moksha Saptami. This day is celebrated on large scale at Parasnath tonk of the mountain, in northern <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jharkhand" title="Jharkhand" id="mw_g">Jharkhand</a>, part of the Parasnath Range<sup about="#mwt131" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKailash_Chand_Jain199113_19-1" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTEKailash Chand Jain199113"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEKailash_Chand_Jain199113-19"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Kailash Chand Jain"},"2":{"wt":"1991"},"p":{"wt":"13"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKailash_Chand_Jain199113-19" id="mw_w"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAQA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAQE">[</span>19<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAQI">]</span></span></a></sup> by offering Nirvana <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laddu" title="Laddu" id="mwAQM">Laddu</a> (Sugar balls) and reciting of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nirvana_Kanda" title="Nirvana Kanda" id="mwAQQ">Nirvana Kanda</a>.</p>

</section><section data-mw-section-id="6" id="mwAQU" aria-labelledby="Santhal_heritage"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Santhal_heritage">Santhal heritage</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Parasnath&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section's source code: Santhal heritage"><span>edit source</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>

<p id="mwAQY">The local Santhal community, which comprises 26% of the population of Jharkhand, use the name <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marang_Buru" title="Marang Buru" id="mwAQc">Marang Buru</a> to refer to both their deity and the mountain.<sup about="#mwt136" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-SorenSingh2024_4-2" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"SorenSingh2024"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-SorenSingh2024-4"}}'><a href="#cite_note-SorenSingh2024-4" id="mwAQg"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAQk"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAQo">[</span>4<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAQs">]</span></span></a></sup> They are in dispute with the Jain community as to certain ancestral worship and other rights, and these have been subject to judicial review historically.<sup about="#mwt139" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Deogharia25_2-1" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Deogharia25"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-Deogharia25-2"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Deogharia25-2" id="mwAQw"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAQ0"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAQ4">[</span>2<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAQ8">]</span></span></a></sup> Recent bans on meat and alcohol, upheld by the Jharkhand High Court in 2025, have exacerbated the conflict, curtailing Santal traditions and fueling protests and counter-petitions in the court.<sup about="#mwt142" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-:0_31-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":":0"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-:0-31"}}'><a href="#cite_note-:0-31" id="mwARA"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwARE"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwARI">[</span>31<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwARM">]</span></span></a></sup> However Jains claim that Santhal claim is implausible and illogical and that actually Parasnath is a Jain pilgrimage since ancient times. They also observe that Jains and Adivasis have co-existed at Parasnath Hill since ancient times, and that when the tirthankaras sought nirvana there, Adivasis traditionally carried them to the summit, a practice that continues today.<sup about="#mwt143" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-:0_31-1" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":":0"}}'><a href="#cite_note-:0-31" id="mwARQ"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwARU"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwARY">[</span>31<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwARc">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

</section><section data-mw-section-id="7" id="mwARg" aria-labelledby="See_also"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="See_also">See also</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Parasnath&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section's source code: See also"><span>edit source</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>

<ul id="mwAUo"><li id="mwAUs"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_mountains_in_India" title="List of mountains in India" id="mwAUw">List of mountains in India</a></li>
